ParlaMint-NL_2016-05-31-eerstekamer-4.u1	I would like to welcome the Minister of Foreign Affairs and the Minister of Defence to the First Chamber. I give the floor to Mr Knapen.
ParlaMint-NL_2016-05-31-eerstekamer-4.u2	Mr. Chairman. We live on a fragile continent. Think about it. All the Member States of the European Union are small by global standards. The amount of natural resources is limited here. The territory is generally difficult to defend; borders are porous. We depend on a global order that we cannot guarantee ourselves. And last but not least, Europe is getting smaller simply because new emerging states outside Europe are getting bigger and more powerful. That is why it is so logical that a common foreign policy was a central theme in the Maastricht Treaty in 1993 and even more a renewed ambition after the great European schism about the American invasion of Iraq in 2003. Pooling forces to deal with these vulnerabilities! Little has come of that, because we were given the opportunity to fool ourselves. We did this mainly with the help of illusions, which have the characteristic of being persistent. Firstly, some Member States in Europe have remained genuinely of the opinion that they are far more important in the international world than is actually the case. Secondly, we have liked to admit to the illusion that the world is primarily benevolent and cooperative. In the opinion of the EU country, it is convinced that Russia will naturally integrate into our open, free architecture of living together. With such a benevolent optimism, it has been possible to eliminate defence budgets for years. Thirdly, everyone has persistently maintained the illusion that, as a matter of fact, the United States will take care of our security, while America has said 'no, thank you' on several occasions, for example in Libya and Syria. Fourthly, in the rays of sunshine, we cherish the idea that the European Union is a moral superpower. We approach the rest of the world with soft power and moral superiority in the certainty that the rest will eventually be no different than us. A fascinating cocktail, in short, of idealism, naivety and illusions. The last European security strategy is back in 2003 and is logically based on such an optimistic and sometimes complacent view of the world. It's all multilateralism that beats the clock. In order to avoid misunderstandings, we are strongly in favour of multilateralism and the Brussels machinery is also good at this. However, we must not turn our backs on a sometimes cold geopolitical reality which, unfortunately, also demands its share in international relations. Now it has to be said that it has gone well for a long time. We can now see that we are paying a price for this. That price is actually always the same in such a situation. After all, the price of weakness is a reduced sovereignty over strategic decisions. The refugee crisis makes the pregnant visible. Those who cannot defend their borders properly, those who have no power to contribute in the burning region to crisis management, safe ports, socio-economic progress and stability, are forced to act out of a position of weakness. The deal with Turkey is, in this respect, one from the book: we pay the price of one's own weakness. Ergo, the European Union bows to power, pays and hopes that it will end well. The next prize is in Africa. In a number of areas of instability, just below the Sahara, there are tiny EU missions to contribute to stabilisation and crisis management. The Netherlands has done an admirable job in Mali and the commitment of this minister is well known and appreciated. From the wider perspective of the Union, it is all ad hoc and without many policies. Last time I asked the Minister about the EU Battlegroups. He didn't answer it at the time. Those battlegroups have been around for almost ten years, at least on paper, but nothing has ever been done with them. One time a country didn't want the cost, the other time a country didn't want to identify with Europe and the next time everyone hoped to let the French take the chestnuts out of the fire. It also happened once that decision-making had been delayed for so long that the facts had overtaken it all. It was about Chad at the time. What does the Dutch Government think about this? Does she see any chances of pulling these battlegroups smoothly? Don't get me wrong: security is not only a matter of sending soldiers on, but can sometimes play a crucial role. Africa is a socio-economic, ideological and sometimes demographic time bomb, certainly a number of large cities. We are now letting China set the tone there in the future. There is no question of European diplomacy as a powerful entity. The third D, that of development, offers the sight of fragmentation. As far as the huge Brussels budgets are concerned, this is an approach that is decoupled from a European interest. We cannot even blame the Brussels development agencies for this, because there is no defined European interest. America has national interests, vital interests, which allow the President to act. Britain knows it too, France knows it too, Italy knows it too, and so you could go on for a while. However, there is no European interest, simply because Europe is not a state. That is, of course, not without consequences. Take the relationship with China. The United States is following a pragmatic, cautious course. They give priority to that region with the "pivot to Asia'. Europe, on the other hand, has no course. Each one travels to China and hopes to return with trade benefits. China is, rightly from its point of view, competent in this respect. It's playing Member States against each other. For example, in Central Europe, through the Investment Forum 16+1, it has given itself a wonderful economic preference. In America it happens sometimes that a powerful Chinese company does not enter, because then America relies on its national interest. Europe never does, because Europe has no national interest; it only has competing Member States. Of course, it doesn't matter to criticize this alone. The point is that we are working on improvements. Does this cabinet see opportunities for this or should we accept for the time being that it is as it is? Our group is curious about the ideas and plans of this cabinet. Our cabinet will be aware that China is currently building the logistics infrastructure for a base in Djibouti. China has also become more active with peace operations in the region. A Chinese white paper states that this is about "effective participation in security cooperation and the safeguarding of Chinese interests overseas." Of course, it is quite right. In itself, this can also be very positive. It also offers opportunities to work together to promote stability in Africa, but again the question is how such a thing should work if Europe cannot be a party because of fragmentation and individual Member States are too small for such a thing. Following the attacks in Paris last November, the French President did not call in NATO's assistance, as America did after 9/11. As you know, Article 5 of the NATO Treaty obliges allies to come to the aid of the NATO partner if the territory is threatened. No, President Hollande referred to the relatively obscure Article 42 (7) of the European Treaty of Lisbon. It states that EU Member States are obliged to provide each other with all possible assistance when one of them is a victim of armed aggression on its territory. The High Representative Mogherini was there as the chickens to swear that this would not imply any EU security mission. How does the Dutch government feel about this? Do this Minister see the French request as an embryonic step towards more European security cooperation or perhaps simply as something typical of French? I would just like to say that my group welcomes this French step. This question is also relevant because aid to France implies a lot. After all, the country is military in many places. It is active in Lebanon, the Horn of Africa, Mali and the Central African Republic. Is there not a duty to increase European burden-sharing now that even the French have found that it is all a bit much for them? Take Lebanon. France has been carrying a heavy burden there for decades. We all know how important that is, because chaos in Lebanon will also immediately put millions of people on the run. I realize that these are uncomfortable issues, but the situation at our southern and eastern borders is not easy. We must also realise that a European security and defence policy is difficult to set up with 28 Member States. There are too many different orientations and interests for this. However, I tend towards optimism when a few countries join forces and point the way. It is conceivable that some small cores form around lead nations such as France or Germany. We would welcome initiatives by the Cabinet to shape and encourage this. We see opportunities in that area. How does the Cabinet see this? Such a development, of course, also requires far-reaching military coordination. Task specialisation is a sensitive issue, which affects the sovereignty of Member States. But it will have to happen that one country focuses more on one specialization while the other is more focused on the other specialization. If not, additional resources for the Defense Department are only additional contributions to fragmentation and not to pooled assets, and thus lost money. America still plays a major role in our security. NATO provides the framework for this. Since Russia's geopolitical ambition is so assertive, NATO is once again a central security anchor for many Member States. But the European Union must also be a party here. After all, the Russian strategy is not one of normal warfare. No, it works with special units from the inside, it undermines integrity, it manipulates minorities, and it uses psychological weapons and cyberattacks. Defence against this requires investment in stability and also in economic resilience. It requires investment in things that are more European than NATO. Let us not fool ourselves: in America, irritation is growing about the profiteering behaviour of European countries. That is why it is good that Europe, and therefore our country, is more concerned about a fair contribution to our own security. Security is more than military resilience. Our destiny and political cohesion in the West can also be strengthened by new connections, for example by a new trade agreement between Europe and America. All kinds of groups get very excited about TTIP, for example when it comes to chlorine chicken, maize and trade arbitrage. That is all right, and that is sometimes extremely sensible, but let us not lose sight of the geopolitical perspective. TTIP is hardly about commercial goods; it is about standards, rules for contracts, socio-economic practices, standards and fair procurement. It is about a final opportunity for Europeans and Americans together to determine how people interact in the global marketplace. If this is not done, rapidly growing countries outside the West will soon be imposing their own standards more and more on the international community. In that sense TTIP is geopolitics of the first order, however much it is possible for us to care about the chicken. On this point, I do have a question for the cabinet. Can it actually be seriously negotiated in Brussels when we in the Netherlands might be able to have the whole thing cancelled by referendum by angry citizens? How seriously do they take the Dutch input in Brussels and Washington as they get what Dutch government positions and parliamentary decisions are really worth. A good example of common European foreign policy is the boycott package against Russia following the annexation of Crimea and the undermining of the Minsk agreements. The division between the Member States is considerable, but so far it has been possible to draw a common line, especially thanks to Germany. My group appreciates this consensus and the contributions made by this cabinet. The effectiveness of the boycott is questionable. I'm curious about the government's judgment. Moreover, it is striking that a small, plutocratic circle around Putin in cities such as London and Vienna continues to benefit unhindered from the security and legal protection of the West. They live well there. Their children benefit from good universities. They give literal and cynical content to the expression "best of both worlds." The current boycott is not exactly a highlight of sophistication in a globalised era. How does the cabinet see the effectiveness and perspective of this boycott? Finally. Today we are talking about security in Europe. There's safe limits to that. We do not need to discuss the refugee debate, but, as I said at the beginning, our borders are porous. There was a rush last summer at a European Border and Coast Guard Agency. The Commission put a plan on the table. We've been three-quarters of a year. I understand the trilogue could start, but ask the cabinet what's going on. When will this Border and Coast Guard Agency be real? Or do we actually keep leaning on the blunt axe of Macedonia, which has simply blocked the passage for everyone? Those are urgent questions at a time when perhaps we are not so shy of an ever closer Union, but all the more so of a stronger Union, capable of standing in the midst of international threats, defending interests and values and protecting citizens. Our group looks forward to the exchange of views with the cabinet.
ParlaMint-NL_2016-05-31-eerstekamer-4.u3	Mr. Chairman. "I've come to the conclusion that a friendly word with a gun in its hand works out more than just a friendly word." With this dry but realistic statement by John Dillinger, I want to use my contribution to international security. The criminal trial against Verviers' terrorists had to be stopped recently. The Belgian security staff demanded better working conditions and therefore refused to work after 16.00 hours. Punctuality actions in the fight against terrorists. Perhaps this simple incident is symptomatic and significant for a somewhat spoiled continent. Western Europe has had 70 years of vacation from history. Peace and security are self-evident and free, we thought. Attacks and violent outbursts near European borders wake us up. But are we really over sleeping drunkenness or is our concern especially that of the whole Holland Bakt generation: as long as I have my apple pie ready? This will prove to be the case if the political parties adopt their defence paragraph in the electoral programme. Perhaps the most important paragraph for the forthcoming elections. Defense is exhausted and it will cost billions more to build a suitable, professional military force. The message that our outdated F-16s cannot be deployed in all circumstances over Syria was not so much staggering, but rather the fact that the House of Representatives were disheartened about it. Are they not aware of the state of our defence equipment? With a slipper and an old football shoe, you can't get very far into this violence spectrum. How is it possible that we have so neglected our defence, the core task of the government? Parliamentary surveys have been carried out on smaller issues. Can the Minister of Defence indicate what significant steps it is taking in the aftermath of this cabinet to stop deficits and gaps as much as possible and thus lay a foundation on which a new cabinet can build? Which building blocks is she going to supply for the formation table? Our defence expenditure is far below the NATO 2% standard. What mechanism is the Minister going to change this, in a way that is structural and not dependent on political lazyness? It hardly needs to be said that we cannot afford any more grievances. Putin's regime will do everything it can to maintain a tsarist system. This means internal continuing repression and foreign continuation of aggressive anti-Western policy. The former Soviet republics, in particular Georgia, Ukraine and Moldova, will continue to undermine Putin politically and militarily in order to prevent and maintain NATO and EU accession. Despite the poor economy, the re-arming will continue. This is not consistent with the ongoing reductions in defence in NATO. Will NATO really take action if the Baltic States are under Russian foot? What is actually available to reverse such a threat? China continues to develop as new economic, political and military power. This leads to conflicts with the US, Japan and other countries in the region, over control of sea routes and islets with raw materials. What is our government doing to prevent a serious armed conflict and to ensure that countries like Taiwan are not intimidated away? Iran has gained more financial strength by lifting the sanctions of the international community to sponsor terrorist groups. There is now plenty of trade with Iran, but what pressure is being put to stop Iran from financing terror? With regard to North Africa and the Middle East, the unrest will continue through IS, Al-Qaeda and all kinds of warlords. ISIS certainly hasn't finished playing yet. The instability in that region will continue. This could threaten oil supplies and generate even more refugee flows. Against all these threats is an EU that hangs on the ropes. Uncertain about her identity, ambushed by the refugee problem and rightly distressed by the domination of radical Islam. Hundreds of people with Western passports are currently part of the IS army and can one day return home to start their personal jihad. By the way, when are Dutch soldiers going to be in uniform again? We can't give in to terrorists forever, can we? It's a bad signal when our swordpower dives away. There is no such ban in France or Belgium, and the threat level is certainly not lower there. I'll follow. A possible Brexit will further undermine Europeans' confidence in the EU. In this list, I also mention Turkey, which, under the leadership of Erdogan, is becoming increasingly radical. A bad partner for the EU and unsuitable for EU membership. I think that's the last thing Erdogan wants. Membership of the EU means that his sultan-like ambitions will become impossible and he will never accept that. In this sense, the biggest brush we can bake him is the direct offering of EU membership. But in all seriousness, let us stop with the comedy of possible accession. Europe stops where the church towers end. Turkey is an important NATO partner. That is why good relations are necessary, but why should this be done through EU membership, I ask the Minister. Mr. Chairman. Combating terrorist attacks is a top priority. Is this also the danger of nuclear terrorism? Isn't this an underexposed subject? In the shadow of the attacks in Belgium, a nuclear dimension was also hidden. It led to a sharp warning from the International Atomic Energy Agency (ILO). Nuclear terrorism poses a real threat, for example from IS. IS has many sympathizers in Arab countries. The nuclear material there is a logical target for them. How will this threat be countered? How can such weapons be prevented from falling into the wrong hands? President Obama placed this item high on his agenda years ago, particularly supported by Israel. But how will this go with his successor? Is the IAEA taking sufficient lead in this explosive file? Speaking of international security, I place my finger on the paralyzed peace process in the Middle East. Palestinian acts of terror are the order of the day in Israel. Glorifying violence against Jews fills Palestinian state television. The Palestinian Authority is still transferring large sums of money to convicted Palestinian terrorists. Abbas recently acknowledged in his conversation with the Norwegian Foreign Minister that the PA is now paying the salaries of terrorists through the PLO. The higher the penalty, the higher the bonus. There are terrorists who receive almost €3,000 each month; a capital in that region. It is insufferable and immoral that the Netherlands and the EU are paying for this on the left or right. Imagine, for example, that in the Netherlands convicted terrorists with Turkish nationality receive large sums of money from the Turkish government and that the EU provides enormous financial support to Turkey every year. Our country would rightly be too small. Why do other criteria apply to the Palestinian Authority? I call on the Minister to deduct €10 from the Dutch donor funds for every euro transferred to terrorists. Meanwhile, the Palestinians refuse to appear at the negotiating table with Israel, because they know that Israel is considered a scapegoat by the international community. They're counting on them getting what they want, even without direct negotiations. What concrete pressure is being put on the Palestinians to start direct negotiations with Israel? The Swiss parliament is going to investigate the government funding of anti-Israeli NGOs with targets that are obstructing official government policy. Will the Minister, like Switzerland, also carry out research to stop these harmful subsidies?

ParlaMint-NL_2016-05-31-eerstekamer-4.u8	Mr. Chairman. This policy debate is about international security. This has to do with armed conflicts between and within states or with their threats, but it also has to do with the question of how to maintain peace through poverty reduction, fair distribution of wealth, political participation, sustainable development, respect for human rights, building and maintenance of the rule of law and good governance. That is, of course, a very broad approach to safety. It involves more than national security, no matter how important it is. It is also about safety for people, about human safety. This already indicates how much internal and external security are intertwined. This broad approach to security is the shared responsibility of national states, international organisations, civil society movements, the private sector and individual citizens. The PvdA wants all efforts to be directed towards the constant pursuit of that shared and human security in a truly turbulent world. This raises the question of what the role of Europe and the role of the Netherlands can be. This will also be the common thread in the five subjects which I will briefly address on behalf of my group. First of all, I would like to say something about the Turkey deal in a broad perspective. The security of the Syrian and Iraqi people is at enormous risk. In Syria, millions of people have been driven out of their homes; several hundred thousand have been killed. The prospects for a structural solution are far from clear. The flow of refugees from this war zone is still continuing. We continue to stand with the government for a generous reception policy here and in the region. The so-called Turkey deal is essential for reception in the region. We must not forget that Turkey has received nearly 3 million Syrians, Lebanon 1.2 million and Jordan nearly 1 million. They're the countries of first shelter. We can see that the arch is incredibly tense there. In addition to continuing humanitarian and financial assistance to Lebanon and Jordan, it remains important to recognise Turkey's crucial role and to focus cooperation on it. Of course, this must not be done at all costs, but my group welcomes the Turkey deal as an important condition for controlling the increasing flow of refugees and for achieving decent reception in Turkey with European aid. Of course, if, under the pretext of the refugee crisis or the fight against terrorism, Turkey is going to hunt down the Kurds and cut down on fundamental freedoms in Turkey, we must appeal to Turkey in particular in the Council of Europe, where Turkey is also party to the European Convention on Human Rights. However, the EU must not always come up with varying demands on Turkey either. It seems to the PvdA Group to be right to depoliticise the refugee issue. We ask the government to respond to this position. In addition, my group informs about the nature and the latest state of affairs of the Turkey deal. Is it, above all, a framework agreement on some 14 A4s between the European Union as a representative of the Member States and Turkey? To what extent are the Member States already bound by this? Or do certain steps still need to be taken to achieve this? And which ones are they? What is the Dutch position here? What would be the consequences for refugee reception and refugee policy, in the region and in Western Europe, if the Turkish deal were to go down unexpectedly? Compliance with the UN Refugee Convention is crucial here. Of course, the text of this treaty is somewhat outdated, but as with many treaties, in a dynamic evolutionary context, the application and interpretation are now based on the contemporary meaning. That doesn't include a word of French. These are people who, out of well-founded fear of their safety and life, eventually feel forced to leave their familiar surroundings. The Convention does not cover economic migrants. In addition, the UN Refugee Convention is a global treaty. The operation of the Convention is not limited to refugees from Europe. Does the government share these two principles? It is now five years since the Arab Spring sparked a spark of hope for freedom and democracy. But in a lot of Arab countries...
ParlaMint-NL_2016-05-31-eerstekamer-4.u9	Do I understand correctly that the PvdA Group believes that the Turkey deal is fully in line with the UN Refugee Treaty and that it is in no way sanding? Is that a good impression?
ParlaMint-NL_2016-05-31-eerstekamer-4.u10	There is a slight inconvenience in the sense that Turkey itself is not a full party to the UN Refugee Convention. However, in the practice of Turkish policy, it is largely applied. As you know, the Turkey deal includes 72 agreements between the EU and Turkey. This includes proper treatment of refugees. This will not always be done entirely according to the standards of the Procedural Directive applied in the European Union, but Turkey is not a member of the European Union and respects the spirit of the UN Refugee Treaty.
ParlaMint-NL_2016-05-31-eerstekamer-4.u11	Is Mr Schrijver aware that there are several reports about the treatment of returned Syrian refugees in Turkey, and that this is not always in line with the letter and spirit of the UN Refugee Convention? Does he know that there have also been Greek judges who have questioned the legal possibility of sending Syrian refugees back to Turkey?
ParlaMint-NL_2016-05-31-eerstekamer-4.u12	Yes. With you, we are well aware of this and we are concerned about it. That is why we want to exert as much influence as possible on Turkey. And we don't do that by isolating them, but by seeking cooperation. That is, miracle by miracle, laid down in the Turkey deal. Implementation has many problems, but we are in favour of solving these problems and not now reducing cooperation to zero.
ParlaMint-NL_2016-05-31-eerstekamer-4.u13	Finally, Mr Van Apeldoorn.
ParlaMint-NL_2016-05-31-eerstekamer-4.u14	One last question about this. Is Mr Schrijver, in the light of current political developments in Turkey, the relationship with the Kurds, the position of Kurdish MPs and Erdogan's reaction to certain elements of the Turkey deal, optimistic about the future cooperation with Turkey in the implementation of the Turkey deal?
ParlaMint-NL_2016-05-31-eerstekamer-4.u15	Thank you for your question. I'm not optimistic about that, but I'm realistic. If we do not seek cooperation with Turkey and try to exert our influence through cooperation between the EU and Turkey, through the Council of Europe and through other bodies, the situation will be worse. I'm going back to the Middle East. I said that in quite a few Arab countries, the Arab Spring has unfortunately degenerate into a fiery sea, such as in Libya, Yemen and Syria, or eventually one dictatorship has been redeemed by another, as in Egypt. Here is the bitter lesson that freedom and democracy can be better achieved along the path of graduality and must be firmly rooted in society in order to sustain it. Long-standing involvement of the international community, through development projects and strengthening the rule of law, can play an important role. This is a long-term issue, as experience in Afghanistan and Iraq, for example, teaches us. It raises the question of whether the free Western world can take that long, and it also shows the reduced development and defence efforts of a number of Western countries. What is the Dutch government's position on this? The root cause of the refugee problem is the hopelessness of shared peace and human security, particularly in Syria. How does the government see the peace talks unfold? The existence of the International Syria Support Group is a promising sign, but it does consist of a furious group of countries with very different interests. How does the government assess the role and relations between key players, such as Russia, the US, China and Iran? And how does it see the role of the EU in this, including that of High Representative Mogherini himself? Only a fully-fledged and comprehensive international peace strategy for Syria can bring relief in the long term. To what extent is this already negotiable? Or is all the attention claimed by the urgency of everyday life about achieving a ceasefire, the delivery of humanitarian aid and a dialogue between the Syrian parties? And to the extent that it is already possible to talk about post-war peace-building, how could it best be organised? The United Nations, including UNICEF and UNDP, and its specialised organisations such as World Bank, FAO and WHO are likely to play a crucial role in this. Are Member States, including the Netherlands, preparing these organisations for such a role? We have already held extensive written consultations with the government on the legal basis for military action in Iraq and Syria following the Article 100 letters. Bearing in mind the role of the First Chamber, we have focused this consultation on the underlying legal framework. My group thanks the government for the detailed answer. It is clear that there is a special patchwork of legal bases, which partly overlap. Iraq's right to self-defense plays an important role in this and extends to Syria's territory, where the ISIS command centres in Syria and the supply lines of combat units and ammunition from Eastern Syria threaten Iraq. At the same time, we also see that the Assad regime has invoked the aid of Russia and Iran on the basis of the Syrian right to self-defense. In turn, Turkey also calls for the right to self-defense to oppose armed attacks from Syria's territory; unfortunately, the difficult Kurdish issue is a poisonous issue; we have already talked about it. We are not going to repeat that detailed written consultation here orally. My group does, however, have some additional questions. These questions relate essentially to the question of how the right to self-defense, ultimately a form of self-direction, can be found in the UN's collective security trail. For example, my group had requested an appreciation from the government of the additional, possible legitimising value that could be derived from paragraph 5 of Security Council Resolution 2249 of 20 November 2015, which was unanimously adopted after the attacks in Paris. It has not been translated into the standard language required by the Security Council for an international mandate for the use of force, but it is moving in that direction. Can this resolution, together with others, including the important resolution 2254 dealing with an integral peace package for Syria, be seen as an opportunity, perhaps just a straw, to come from the trail of collective self-defense of Iraq, the current legal basis for Dutch military action, in our view much better track of collective security within the framework of the UN? This requires cooperation, including with Russia, Iran and China. We are happy to give the government an opportunity to reply to this. I also ask the Minister of Defence to update on the military deployment of our F-16s in Syria. Mr Van Dijk already referred to the fact that the precision of our F-16s was initially greatly praised, which was the basis of the request from the French and Americans to the Netherlands to extend our aerial bombings to Syria, while we later received reports that this was largely absent because the communication equipment was not sufficiently set up. The key question is, of course, whether the actions against ISIS are effective. Or is it the bitter lesson that a harnessed armed group such as ISIS and a fanatical ideology about a caliphate to be founded will not be bombed out of the sky? My question to the government is whether we do not actually walk around with our hands tied on our backs because, ultimately, only a comprehensive peace strategy, with its military components air bombardments and ground forces, and fully embedded in that peace strategy, and not in parallel with that, has at best any chance of success? Even closer to home is the conflict area in Ukraine. Fortunately, part of the ceasefire on the basis of Minsk II appears to be sustained. In doing so, the OSCE Peace Monitors, approximately 1000 staff, play an unarmed role as an important observer. In our previous debate in 2015, the Upper House adopted by a large majority a PvdA motion to improve the legal status of the OSCE. We are pleased that the government, after initially a rather harsh response in December, in January 2016 discussed this motion in detail. We are deeply aware of the contradictions, particularly between Russia and the United States, but the OSCE is an organisation of 57 Member States and even more so than the others, in order not to allow Europe to be held hostage by the East-West conflict. We now know that the German Presidency will also give priority to this issue. Can the government give us an update in this debate on the state of play and also on the Dutch commitment to it? When we talk about Ukraine, of course, I also come to the Dutch response to the outcome of the referendum. I believe that all of us are genuinely concerned about the contribution that Europe can make to peace, social progress, the rule of law and democracy in Ukraine. Our view that the EU-Ukraine Association Agreement is an important instrument in this regard has not changed since we had an exchange of views with the government on this matter on 7 July 2015 and voted in favour of the Act of Approval. Unlike both chambers, a clear majority of the participants in the consultative referendum voted against it. We think it is sensible that the government, on the basis of this statement, should not be able to proceed 'to ratification'. We would like to hear from the government how consultations with the European partners and the European Commission are going on on this issue. At what time does the government intend to take decisions? At some point in time, the government will have to give in, now that the ratification procedures have been completed in our country after all the treaty partners. In my group's view, there are various legal methods to ensure that the result of the referendum is properly taken into account. The most obvious method is to make reservations in some parts of the agreement. For example, the Netherlands may declare that it is expressly not bound by specific Treaty provisions. The Association Agreement itself does not prohibit this and, under the general law of the Treaties, it is possible to make a reservation, provided that it does not conflict with the purpose and scope of the Convention. A less far-reaching method is to make interpretative statements on how the Netherlands understands certain treaty articles as a contracting party. My question to the government is how it stands here. Of course, the most far-reaching method is, of course, repealing the ratification law. My group is not in favour of this, given the great interests of shared peace, stability and the building of the rule of law and democracy at stake here for Ukraine and Europe. Moreover, we do not need Putin to laugh in his face. We are awaiting the publication of the new European Security Strategy of the EU High Representative Federica Mogherini.

ParlaMint-NL_2016-05-31-eerstekamer-4.u33	I would like to thank Mr Van Kappen for this question. I honestly believe that the comparison with the fight against crime is flawed, for two reasons. ParlaMint-NL_2016-05-31-eerstekamer-4.u70	Mr. Chairman. My first rule was: the world hasn't gotten any clearer. A more classical form of security policy is the security policy aimed at conflict prevention and conflict management and post-conflict reconstruction, for example in the case of civil wars and fragile states that break up. In these cases, a broad, comprehensive approach is needed bringing together military, political and economic issues. My group therefore welcomes the fact that the cabinet has often called the integrated approach "3D" in our country: defence, diplomacy and development; we have taken that term from the Canadians into one of the priorities of its international security strategy. Against this background, I do have a question about the Dutch participation in the UN mission in Mali and the current discussion about whether or not to end that participation. As the Cabinet points out in its note on the integrated approach, the difference in time horizon between the military and civil use of resources is one of the key problems of that approach. If possible, military commitment must be as short as possible, but for reconstruction and development cooperation one must think in long-term terms. This often requires security. There is therefore a tension between what the military would initially want, namely not to stay in one place for too long, and the situation which requires the military not to leave too early. What does that mean for the Dutch military contribution to the UN mission in Mali? The cabinet is currently examining the possibility of extending that contribution. It's been going on for a while. Apparently, there is a certain gap within the coalition and the two parties have opposite views, as we sometimes see on other foreign policy issues. I understand that financial aspects and the practical capacity to continue the mission play an important role for the Minister of Defence. But there are also other aspects mentioned by the cabinet itself in the memorandum on the integrated approach. The main question is how is it ensured that the withdrawal of the Dutch military from a particular conflict area, which is the case in this case, will therefore provide Mali with the security necessary for reconstruction and development cooperation. I understand that this is a prerequisite for the cabinet. It's also recorded in the note. How does the cabinet stand in this discussion? How does it weigh up the different interests and perspectives between military and non-military in the specific case of Mali? In addition, Mali has been an important partner of the Netherlands in the field of development cooperation for decades. In this respect, it is an excellent candidate for an integrated approach, possibly including a longer military contribution than the Ministry of Defence would like. I am particularly interested in the view of the Foreign Minister on this, because he has had a great deal of practical experience with the situation in Mali. Can he explain this? Sixteen UN peacekeeping operations are currently under way. There are 105,000 soldiers and policemen active there. So that is not as the British say "small bear" and also a further 15,000 civilian personnel. They are of great value for the prevention and management of conflicts. It is not for nothing that the UN peacekeeping operations were awarded the Nobel Prize in 1988. At the same time, the way in which the UN prepares and carries out its peacekeeping operations is often inadequate. I say that with pain in the heart. The report of the UN Independent Panel on Peace Operations a year ago, for example, points to the discrepancy between the often ambitious mandates, in which all kinds of tasks are accumulated for the military, and the resources made available, which are often less than those described by the UN as necessary. The UN organisation depends on the contributions of the Member States. So you would expect some kind of correction to be made between objectives and means, a trooper-to-task analysis as it is called in military jargon, but it does not take place. As a result, missions are sometimes doomed to failure, given the assignment they have received. In addition, there is micromanagement in New York and insufficient consultation with the countries supplying troops. The selection of persons in leadership positions is insufficient. According to this panel, more women should also be given heavy positions in UN peacekeeping operations. This last point is in line with UN Resolution 1325. Women, Peace and Security is one of the themes in the Dutch campaign for the Security Council. It is therefore a good thing that the Netherlands has made the choice to bring this particular element forward in its campaign. All in all, the current approach to peacekeeping operations is almost irresponsible in a number of respects. In my view, concrete improvements in the way the UN deals with military matters are necessary. My group therefore hopes that the panel report will lead to the necessary improvements set out in this report. What is the position of the Netherlands and the EU in the debate on the follow-up to this report? At the same time, the Panel stresses the need to find political and non-military solutions to conflicts in order to achieve a sustainable outcome. That's important. Mr Van Apeldoorn will be satisfied with this observation. Forced peace by military means, also known as "enforcement," strongly rejects the panel, in line with the line of thought "the UN is peace, not war." However, there are some reservations about the approach taken by the panel. Does this mean that the gradual development of recent years will disappear in order to implement more "robust peacekeeping"? However, the military are more forceful and more willing to use military means where necessary. The UN rule on peacekeeping missions was only to use force in the form of defence, so if you are attacked. In this new notion of "robust peacekeeping," for example, you should take offensive action in the protection of the civilian population at some point. What about the Netherlands in the discussion on the role of military and the degree of "robust peacekeeping"? I'm here to talk about Russia. The subject has just been discussed in detail. Our discussion today dates back to the letter from the Cabinet of June 2013 on the international security situation. In that letter, a distinction was made between three strategic interests of our country, namely the defence of its own and federal territory, a functioning international legal order and economic security. All three come together in the relationship with Russia. Moscow's decision to invade Ukraine, annex Crimea and launch a rebellion in eastern Ukraine is a direct violation of the international legal order. Our economic security is under pressure from Russian cyber espionage and cyber attacks. Moreover, it is the government's ambition to position the Netherlands as the gas roundabout of Northwest Europe, through the import of Russian gas. It doubled between 2010 and 2015. Shouldn't there be careful consideration of whether we are becoming too dependent on the supplies of gas from Moscow? We are therefore making ourselves vulnerable to pressure from Russia. Like our colleagues in the House of Representatives, we are therefore looking forward to the geopolitical energy strategy that the Minister for Foreign Affairs will draw up. Can the Minister give us a tip of the veil today? In what direction does the new energy strategy go towards dependence? As regards the third strategic interest mentioned by the cabinet, the defence of the NATO territory and its own territory, the situation has changed completely with the Russian attack on Ukraine and the annexation of Crimea. In my view, the central point is not that Putin is aiming for a Russian sphere of influence along his borders, that he believes that Crimea is actually part of Russia or that he is pursuing an anti-Western policy. The central point is that he has been prepared to use military means to do so, sometimes openly, sometimes concealed in a tactic of hybrid warfare. For the first time since the Second World War, we see a country attacking another country militarily in order to achieve specific objectives, such as annexation of a piece of land in this case. We have thus entered a new dangerous reality, because serious account must be taken of Russian military aggression elsewhere. How unlikely we felt not so long ago, after Crimea we really have to look at this differently. Military aggression against perhaps even NATO countries immediately affects us as an ally of those countries. Moreover, it is worrying that Putin and his closest associates not only talk loosely about an attack on the Eastern European NATO countries, he made a careless remark to the President of Ukraine that Russian troops could be in Kiev in two days and in Warsaw in a week, but that there is also talk about the possible deployment of nuclear weapons. They would be de-escalating because, if Russia were to use a nuclear weapon, the West would not want or be able to react. This would have ended the conflict in Russia's favour, and then de-escalation would have taken place. A bit strange definition of de-escalation, but it is seriously brought forward by the Russians in discussions about a possible conflict and their strategy. It is therefore understandable that NATO countries in Eastern Europe, in particular Poland and the Baltic States, are seriously concerned about possible Russian aggression. The reaction of the alliance has so far been twofold. On the one hand, strengthen the alliance's ability to react quickly and adequately in the event of a crisis through an improved and strengthened NATO Response Force. On the other hand, strengthening the military presence of the allies on the territory of the Eastern European NATO countries. The Dutch armed forces contribute to this in both ways, I note with appreciation. By the way, an addition to the previous discussion on NATO troops in Eastern Europe as part of the deal with Russia on NATO enlargement. A point to keep an eye on. It's not imposed on Russia. A deal has been made about it in the sense that NATO has said: we understand your concern about NATO troops closer to your border, so we promise that we will not permanently station fighters in large numbers in the new NATO countries. This means that at the moment there is a large strategic gap on the east side of the alliance. There is hardly any military presence of NATO and there are many, in some cases even hundreds of thousands of Russian troops on the other side of the border. How can you say that NATO is moving towards Russia and threatening Moscow? It's more like the reverse situation. However, the question arises as to what extent and for how long can Defence continue to follow this path, the path of strengthening its presence in Eastern Europe, given the financial problems for the Ministry and the serious criticism by the General Court of the management of the department. I would be pleased if the Secretary of Defense would respond to that. By the way, military reinforcement isn't the whole story. More is needed, especially confidence-building measures, such as more transparency on exercises and troop relocations and agreements on "no surprises," as it is called: no surprises. We need to know what another person is doing and what we can expect in the military field in terms of presence, exercises and so on. I know that the discussions in Vienna are still on this subject, but I would like to ask the Ministers to continue actively to look for ways to resume the talks and to make proposals to that effect together with other countries in NATO and Vienna. Please respond from the Minister. In general, it is difficult to predict how our relationship with Russia will develop. Since the collapse of the Soviet Union, I believe that Russia has been in an identity crisis. It has borders that it has never had before, with millions of ethnic Russians living in other countries. It has a political system that it has never had before and that is fundamentally unstable. It has an economic system that it has never had before and the weaknesses with falling oil and gas prices become more apparent by the day. While Putin argued in a speech in the Duma ten years ago that Russia was developing into a free and democratic state with European values, in recent years he has increasingly distanced himself from those values and described Russia as an Eurasian country with its own identity and own values. In the past, especially right after the fall of the Wall and the break-up of the Soviet Union, there was a certain tendency not to take Russia fully seriously anymore. It was more or less put away as a loose case, a kind of hopeless third world country with nuclear weapons, which drove on its oil and gas revenues, a country that we didn't have to pay much attention to, except that we liked to buy some energy from it. For years we have seen Russia on the margins of our foreign policy. Now the question of how to deal with Russia is another central question in our foreign policy and I am talking about more than just Russia's threat to our security. In my group's view, last year's Russia letter from the Minister of Foreign Affairs is a good start for a new, mature and more developed Russia policy, which strikes a new balance between de-escalation, dialogue and functional cooperation, as has been the case recently in the Syria crisis, on the one hand, and clear and firm positions where necessary, on the other. My group is particularly concerned about the lack of respect for human rights. The sanctions imposed against Russia for the attack on Ukraine and the annexation of Crimea still have a place in D66's view in such a combination of pressure and dialogue. As far as we are concerned, the debate on possible relaxation of sanctions is centred on the implementation of the Minsk II agreement, with the main point being the restoration of the Ukrainian government's control over its own borders in the eastern part of the country. By the way, the fighting in eastern Ukraine is still continuing, although it is on a limited scale. How do the ministers see this? Do they see any possibility of progress in the Minsk process in the coming months, in particular for the restoration of Ukrainian control over its own borders? I'm going to Syria. Despite a somewhat casual cynicism about the possibility of starting a political process in the civil war in Syria, this has been somewhat unexpected in recent months, at least until recently, because things have been looking a little worse lately. As a first result, agreement was reached on a very fragile and limited-respected truce. In the past and failed attempts to negotiate between the warring parties, the Netherlands played a not insignificant supporting role, because our country accounted for a large part of the budget for the UN negotiators. In other areas too, our country has played a relevant role in the Middle East crisis in recent years. I am thinking of the considerable humanitarian aid that is being given again this year, of our participation in the fight against ISIS in Iraq and Syria, and last but not least of our co-chairship of the Global Counter Terrorism Forum. This has certainly contributed to the fact that the Netherlands has been invited by the US to participate in the meetings of the Syrian Support Group, which plays an important role in coordinating the development of the international community in the Syrian civil war. It seems to me to have a good example of influence by taking responsibility. Two weeks ago, the Foreign Minister took part for the first time in such consultations. Would he like to go into that a little more detail today? I don't want him to tell all of us the greatest secrets collectively. What is his picture of the effectiveness and composition of this company, precisely in the light of the need to launch new attempts? At the moment, things are worse than they are in the right direction. In terms of the influence and role of the international community in the Syria crisis, we are talking about the UN, Russia and the US, but where is Europe? Honesty requires recognition that Europe hardly plays a role in the Syria crisis. That is strange, because it is in the immediate vicinity of the European Union. Europe hardly plays a military role, nor does it play a political role. Only in humanitarian aid is the EU a major player. Will the Foreign Minister, also on the basis of his experience two weeks ago, address the question of the role of the EU in the peace process? The EU is a member of the Syria Support Group, where the Netherlands also participates. Germany, France, the United Kingdom and Italy are, too. It can be said that this makes it difficult for Mrs Mogherini to decide a place of her own, but that does not necessarily have to be the case. See the EU's prominent role in the negotiations with Iran on its nuclear programme. Then why is the EU so absent politically in the Syria crisis? What has been the Dutch attitude to encourage Mrs Mogherini to play a greater role in Syria's drama? I also ask this because the 2013 cabinet letter on the international security strategy refers to "more European responsibility" as the first priority. In the Syrian crisis, at least until now, there is little evidence of this greater European responsibility, and so it is a conflict in our immediate vicinity. My group therefore expects the Minister to make the most of a greater role for the European Union in the peace process. In the press, statements by European diplomats who are frustrated about the dominant role of the US and Russia and advocate multilateralisation of the process appear, in particular a greater role for Europe. I would like to receive a response from the Minister. The civil war in Syria, which has been going on for five years, is causing increasing problems, including for countries that have not so far been sucked into the conflict. I am thinking in particular of Lebanon and Jordan. The EU's small role in the Syrian drama could, as it were, be offset by a European key role in stabilising and supporting these two countries. Lebanon and Jordan have both absorbed very large numbers of refugees and are threatening to succumb to this burden. The latter would greatly increase instability in the Middle East and lead to even greater humanitarian tragedies. In my group's view, therefore, Lebanon and Jordan deserve special treatment which goes beyond the support that is already being given. What opportunities does the cabinet see for this at European level? This could take the form, for example, of a privileged partnership with the European Union, which would provide a framework for exceptional trade opportunities, macro-financial support and cooperation in the fight against terrorism. At national level, our country could upgrade both countries to partner countries in development cooperation. Speaking of the neighbouring countries of Syria, we still have a single question on the Palestinian question. Is there any progress in trying to reach an agreed solution? I think I know there is no such thing, but I hope the Minister will be able to correct me on this point. In any case, a Middle East Conference will be held in Paris on 3 June. I ask the Minister not only what our expectations are, if we have them, but also whether our country is involved. After all, we are the President of the European Union, but apart from that, we have been involved in the debate on the position of the European Union in the Middle East for many years. I would like an answer from the Minister. As far as Turkey is concerned, I should like to confine myself today to the point that our relationship with that country presents us with difficult choices. On the one hand, the autocratic development initiated by President Erdogan is a bad thing and contrary to our views on democracy and human rights, which must have its consequences for the talks with Turkey on membership of the European Union. On the other hand, Turkey is a NATO ally, bordering Russia, with a strategic position between Europe and the Arab world, whose cooperation we need to control the refugee crisis. For the moment, the best alternative still seems to be to keep Turkey as close to the Western world as possible, without compromising on essential values. A greater international role of the European Union and its Member States also requires, in the opinion of D66, more European defence cooperation. Last year an interesting and important report was published by the Centre for European Policy Studies on this subject. It makes a number of sensible recommendations which would represent a major step forward together. I would like to mention four of these recommendations. 1. A permanent EU military headquarters in Brussels. 2. A larger common budget. 3. Establishment of a core group of Member States willing to cooperate further. 4. Closer cooperation between EU Member States in the procurement of defence equipment. Some of these points have already been addressed in previous interventions. All this should result in a European Defence Union, which, among other things, assists NATO in the defence of the alliance territory. The D66 Group supports these proposals, which were made by the Centre for European Policy Studies working group and in which two former Secretary-General of NATO participated, Javier Solana and our fellow countryman Jaap de Hoop Scheffer. So it is difficult to come up with the argument that we are undermining NATO with this European choice. Would the Ministers please respond to these proposals, and hopefully in a positive way? In conclusion, we have a few words to say about the Dutch defence policy. In addition to political considerations, of course, financial considerations are also in favour of more European defence cooperation. The total defence budgets of the 28 EU Member States are now around 190 billion. According to a report by the European Parliament, no less than 26 billion of this is due to fragmented material policy. That is unacceptable in a time of great financial tightness in the defense. The savings that would result from a common European defence equipment policy could then be used for the much-needed reinforcement of the fighting force of the armed forces. In my group's view, first of all, the most basic needs, such as sufficient supplies, parts and ammunition, should be met in the short term. The Court's recent report states that there is a imbalance in this respect between what the Ministry of Defence's support services perform and what the missions in which the Netherlands participate require. The discussion with the Minister on the functioning of her ministry seems to me to be primarily a matter for the House of Representatives, but in the context of our discussion today I would like to discuss the link between a Ministry which is not functioning properly and our country's capacity to participate in international missions. The Secretary of Defense says we should be able to handle it. Of course, but such a statement will come in a different light if the conclusion that we cannot handle it is based on a serious flaw in the logistics process at the department, as the General Court has pointed out. Due to this imperfection, a large proportion of defence equipment is only available in limited quantities. My first question to the Minister is to what extent her reluctance to extend the mission in Mali is linked to the problems in the logistics chain, as indicated in the report of the General Court of Auditors. Secondly, what consequences can these problems have for the current deployment of Dutch units in NATO countries at the eastern border of the alliance? I hope that the consequences will not be too great, because it would really be an unacceptable conclusion that internal dysfunction in The Hague will not enable us to meet our allies' obligations. The above discussion about what our country can or can't handle is in stark contrast to what the government officially uses as the level of ambition for the armed forces. That's not bad. I quote: ambition of the cabinet is a military force prepared for a range of deployments, at all stages of the conflict and at all levels of violence, if necessary at a great distance from our national borders. With regard to the issue of 'at all levels of violence', I would like to see the extent to which this can be implemented within the current financial frameworks. With regard to the issue of "at a great distance from our borders," I wonder whether that does not contradict what the cabinet said in the 2013 letter on international security policy. This letter sets out as one of the guidelines for our foreign and security policy: more focus on unstable regions near Europe. Not "at a great distance," but "near Europe." That is, by the way, a choice that my group supports. This does not rule out the possibility of providing niche capabilities for certain military operations on a limited scale, even far from home, depending on availability at the time. But that is quite different from being able to act at all levels of violence worldwide, as defined by the Ministry of Defence. Mr Van Apeldoorn.

ParlaMint-NL_2016-05-31-eerstekamer-4.u81	Madam President. Last Sunday, in Verdun, a great commemoration took place, with 4,000 young French and Germans, of the Battle of Verdun, 100 years ago. This was done in the presence of President Hollande and Chancellor Merkel. Kohl and Mitterrand preceded them in 1984. The future has a long past: there has been peace for 71 years. I started my reflections in February in the European Reviews as follows. In the last 50 to 60 years we have had a Europe as intended by the founders: wanting to be dependent on each other and working together. Now the self-interest seems to prevail over the common interest and anger towards each other arises. We see a Brexit, the opposite in Europe of the former Eastern bloc vis-à-vis Western Europe and economic differences between northern and southern Europe. I would like to add today the Turkey-Europe deal on refugees, the election results in Austria, new elections in Spain and a provisional advance on the Greek debt mountain; we have to come up with up to 10 billion in the summer. Right-wing and left-wing parties in the center are getting worse and worse with strong growth of extreme right-wing and extreme left-wing parties. Look at Austria, Greece, Spain and recently Baden Württemberg in Germany. These are destabilising developments that can also have an impact on security, both global and European. I read the interview of Minister Schäuble in the Financial Daily last Friday. The headline was: link between low interest rates and increasing populism. I believe that security is also a political issue, even low interest rates. I quote from the article: 'The rise of populist parties worries Schäuble'. The fact that more and more Europeans distrust their governments calls the German Finance Minister one of Europe's greatest challenges. In Germany, confidence in the press is also falling. "That's really dangerous," he notes. However, he sees an opportunity to take the wind out of right-wing populist parties such as Alternative für Deutschland: the ECB must raise interest rates. A new spring, a new sound. Monetary policy, he says, is now causing great concern to many people in this country, Germany. Schäuble sees a link between the extremely reduced interest rate by the ECB and the growing populism. You can't fight that connection, he says to the FD. And: 'As a member of the Federal Government, I have the responsibility to point out that connection'. Otherwise people will feel abandoned." The article also states that the hardliner Schäuble denies that he criticises the generous policy of the ECB. "I am not taking a stand on the ECB's monetary policy. Schäuble would also have said to Draghi, according to the NRC Handelsblad of 14 April: Be proud, you can attribute 50% of the results of that successful party in Germany to this monetary policy. Of course, he was referring to the AfD. This is populism a la Schäuble, I would say. He, who has been in the center of power for so long, indicates what lives among the people. And we have to take that seriously. He felt that Europe was being blamed for everything: the flow of refugees, terrorism, money to Greece, the power of the ECB and the low interest rate policy, the criticism of the euro and also the fight in the United Kingdom about "in or out" Europe. Our argument is that low risk-free interest rates are not only financially risky, but also politically. The low interest rate is risky and, in our opinion, is a political time bomb. German savers are becoming increasingly poor and Dutch pensioners and workers are not indexed, but even reduced on their pensions. The account of the financial crisis is placed at the bottom of society, in the Netherlands with high burdens and cuts until 2016. What does the Spanish government do? It is a pleasure to announce a tax cut in the run-up to the elections in four weeks' time. And what does the European Commission do? It postpones a fine for violating the Stability Pact until after the elections. The reason? You can guess: the populist parties take the wind out of the sails. The Stability Pact has now almost definitively been carried over to the grave. I told you, everything's political. It is remarkable that Germany and the Netherlands are attacking the ECB. These two countries see the independence of the ECB as an issue of faith. The people make a connection between all these issues: we are not being listened to, they are doing so. Major profits from populist parties weaken the political basis for a European solution to the refugee problem in the coming year. That's how we get into a circle. Europe must resolve the refugee crisis, but it loses its support for populism. Debilitating governments or cabinets of three or more parties will be less and less able to cope with this. Now I am addressing the Minister of Defence specifically and I come to the relationship between Russia and NATO. This signal is Russia's response to Obama's decision this year to substantially strengthen the deployment of, among other things, heavy weapons to NATO countries in Central and Eastern Europe. It is intended to discourage Russia from showing further aggression in that region. With those jets, Russia shows its anger at the increase in US military presence in a region that sees it as part of its backyard. Anaconda will be NATO's largest military operation in June: 25,000 troops from 24 NATO countries. Would the Minister care to comment on this? ParlaMint-NL_2016-05-31-eerstekamer-4.u82	Mr. Chairman. Over the last few centuries, Europe has experienced two periods of relative peace: the time of the Metternich restoration after the Wener Congress and the period of the Cold War. Neither were ideal, but both were better than war. During the nineteenth-century peace period, Europe was still dominated by the Vielvölkerimperia, but there were nationalist movements everywhere that completely changed the European map after the first and partly after the Second World War. After the Cold War, the remaining national problems broke out, especially in the Caucasus and the Balkans. Europe now seems more or less at ease and has been able to form a new European Vielvölkerimperium, without compulsion and with, in principle, its own place at the table for each participating nation. But the last nations within the community that do not yet have their own place at that table, the regional minorities or sometimes even majorities in the last multi-population states of Europe, are now studying the possibilities of getting their own place. In the meantime, the European Union of freedom and justice is in danger of almost succumbing under the influence of new nationalism. The people of Europe seem to find it difficult to imagine a different form of state than one on an ethnic basis. We have for a moment thought that, giving room for all cultural diversity, a supranational European people could emerge, but despite the fact that Europe's boundless freedoms are eager to be consumed, it does not seem to be successful. As far as I'm concerned, that's too bad. This divided Europe must adopt an attitude towards the major world problems directly around us, the Middle East, Russia's assertive Russia and Africa's backwardness. There are many things we agree on here in this house and with the government, and also with the rest of Europe, probably more or less. The world is unsafe. It is therefore necessary to restore defence capacity, including our country. Much money is needed to return to the required 2% of GDP, but this may be a high priority. NATO, the Atlantic alliance, remains essential, but I believe that a more European military integration policy is also very important. Several speakers have already mentioned this. As far as I am concerned, in addition to a joint procurement policy, this includes the joint support of a European defence industry through joint purchasing decisions, from an efficiency point of view and also from a strategic precautionary principle. In the extreme case, a good neighbor is more important than a distant friend. Do the ministers agree? The Kurdish problem is an ethnic problem, which is in principle comparable to the nationality problems of old Europe. It is about Poland's situation before the First World War. They were divided into three countries, the Kurds in four, none of which they could regard as their own nation state. In general, the Arab population seems to derive its identity at least as much from its faith as from an ethnic or politically determined given. In practice, they are often strictly separate groups with different political choices. ParlaMint-NL_2016-05-31-eerstekamer-4.u84	Mr. Chairman. The words of Dickens from his famous book Tale of two cities: "It was the best of times and it was the sausage of times," I think give an appropriate description of the turbulent and confusing time we are living in now. We have never been so good, but at the same time we are living in a time of geopolitical shocks and disruptive upheavals. The question is whether future generations will be as good as we are. The international security situation is deteriorating rapidly and is also more complex than during the Cold War. We are facing not only the increased threat to Europe's eastern border due to the rising tensions with Russia, but also the great instability of Europe's southern flank through the disintegration of the Middle East and parts of Africa and the associated mass migration flows. The rise of ISIS and other extremist non-state actors complements this chaotic image. Further from home, we are also facing increasing tensions in Asia due to China's increased assertiveness in the South China Sea. We are also faced with new forms of warfare, such as hybrid and cyber warfare. At the same time, there is economic stagnation, expanding clusters of fragile states, an increase in democracies with autocratic traits and we are in the midst of the fourth industrial revolution. Debates of the European Parliament............................................................................................................................................................................................................................................................ It doesn't make sense to repeat. I shall therefore confine myself to a few points which deserve particular attention in our view. With regard to rising tensions with Russia, comments often compare the time of the Cold War. Understandable, but incorrect. The situation is fundamentally different now. An essential difference is that the Soviet Union did not want to comply with the ruling world order, but instead wanted to impose a different world order based on the communist ideal. Russia, on the other hand, wants to be part of the current world order, but demands a dominant role in it and does not shy away from the use of military force to enforce it. There is also a one-man regime in today's Russia, supported by a new Russian elite of "near friends." This makes the situation more unpredictable and riskier than in the Cold War. The people who surround Putin, the "near friends," are now freely talking about the fact that war with the West is inevitable, because the West is in the process of encircling Russia. In the new Russian doctrine, NATO is therefore referred to as "the enemy." Because Russia does not have the economic stamina to maintain a long-term confrontation with the West, a preventive surprise attack in their eyes is the best option. Even the nuclear map is being put on the table without any hesitation; Mr President has already spoken about it. This is evident not only from the rhetoric, but also from the kind of exercises that are held. Russian aircraft regularly carry out bogus attacks in NATO airspace and many of the scenarios of staff exercises held in Russia end with the deployment of tactical nuclear weapons. It is shocking, but the use of tactical nuclear weapons is seen by the people who surround Putin as de-escalating, because it is assumed that if the NATO countries are faced with the horrible choice to strike back nuclear, the Alliance will throw the towel into the ring and thus bring the conflict to an end. And so, according to this reasoning, the use of tactical nuclear weapons is de-escalating. Moreover, the conflict will soon come to an end and a long-term confrontation will be prevented that Russia cannot sustain. Rightly a terrifying reasoning. The plans to "Ground Launched Cruise Missiles (GLCMs)" with a nuclear charge and a range of 2,500 km. Placed in Kaliningrad and in Crimea, are part of this nuclear blackmail. These missiles are very difficult to intercept. The aim of these weapons systems is not so much to eliminate military targets as to create large numbers of civilian casualties. The comparison with the occasion for the Cuba crisis in October 1962, when we were shaved along the edge of the nuclear abyss, is obvious. The production and installation of these systems is contrary to the "Intermediate-Range Nuclear Forces Treaty" of 1987. It is a sensitive and controversial subject, which I realise too, but unfortunately the nuclear map is back on the table; the nuclear threshold has been lowered. So we are going back in time and NATO's nuclear strategy is in the spotlight again. This strategy is based on the Strategic Concept 2010 and the 2012 Deterrence and Defence Posture Review. A key point is that, although NATO is committed to 'Arms control, disarmament and non-proliferation', NATO is and will remain a nuclear alliance as long as there are nuclear weapons.

The deterrent is based on a balanced mix of conventional and nuclear resources and missile defence systems. One problem is that the "mix" has been disrupted, because due to the cuts in defence over the past few decades, NATO's conventional capacity has been underrated, and there are gaps in missile defence. The strategy of the flexible response used in the Cold War, based on an escalation ladder, is therefore not useful. After all, the ladder has become very short now that a number of steps have been cut off. As a result, an existential threat places more emphasis on nuclear deterrents. In April, a PhD study was published on this subject by Mr Elmar Hellendoorn, of Utrecht University, in which the issue of nuclear blackmail and the lack of an answer to it were raised in a crystal clear manner. Also the article in de Volkskrant of 30 April entitled "Atomic Weapons. Mutual nuclear rhetoric" gives a good picture of the renewed nuclear threat. In order to restore the escalation ladder and thereby be able to respond flexibly to a crisis by conventional means, it is necessary for the Member States of the Alliance to give a high priority to strengthening conventional impact force and missile defence. However, the extensive Cold War instruments to prevent unwanted escalation have been dismantled. The NATO-Russia Council still exists, but is hardly active anymore. ParlaMint-NL_2016-05-31-eerstekamer-4.u94	In the case of deterrents, two components are important: capacity and intent. Sorry for the use of English, but this is the terminology used for this. So you must have the ability to destroy or harm your opponent, and at the same time you must have the political will to use that ability. If you have the ability, but it's obvious you don't have the political will, you don't scare off. If you have the political will, but you don't have the ability to do that, you don't scare off. In the whole nuclear dialogue, reduced to the essence, you will have to make it clear to the Russians that if they ever move to the deployment of tactical nuclear weapon, we will be retiring. At some point, a tactical nuclear weapon was thrown into the assumption, according to the Russian theory, that we would then throw the towel in the ring. The big assignment during this wargame, this tabletop game was: what do we do? In that wargame, the nuclear wargame was knocked back. We made that wargame public. That's part of the curious dialogue you have with each other: that at least you show your opponent that you have the capacity to do so. You have that stuff and that you also have the political will to use it. Doubts as to whether you have the political will are good enough. That's the only way to play that game. If you say now: we throw the nuclear weapons overboard, you'll not only make yourself vulnerable to nuclear blackmail by the Russians, but also by a country like North Korea. They're about ten now. But also a country like Israel has nuclear weapons. Check the Volkskrant, which contains unclassified data. And there are other countries that have nuclear weapons, including in the Middle East. I have indicated what we can do, namely at the point of the escalation ladder. If we want to push that point further forward, we, like all other NATO countries, will have to pay attention to strengthening our conventional defence and our missile defence. That's how you fix the escalator ladder. Mr. Chairman. In the strategic analysis, there is a remarkable division. We must not only devise a strategy aimed at finding an answer to the question of how to deal with Russia's strength, but also a strategy that answers the question of how to deal with Russia's weakness. Security experts have long pointed out that Russia has a number of significant features of a failing state. This theme is now also emerging in the public domain. In an article in the Economist of 1 August 2015, entitled "If Russia breaks up; the peril beyond Putin," it is stated that this is indeed a conceivable scenario. The question is, in that case, who will take control of the Russian nuclear arsenal? This is really a nightmare scenario. There will then be a virtually unmanageable security risk at the eastern border of Europe.
