Strange but true, Mr. Chairman. Our question to the Foreign Minister is whether he sees this danger. Or can he assure us that this is a scenario that only plays in the nightmares of security experts? Apart from Putin's Russia, we must also focus our strategic attention on the southern flank of Europe, where our security is threatened by the disintegration of the Middle East and parts of Africa. So we are forced to spread our strategic focus over two different strategic theatres, each of which requires a different political-military concept in response. That is complicated enough in itself, but the complexity is increased by the Russian intervention in Syria, which links and connects these two theatres. A solution to this region is no longer conceivable without Russia's cooperation, and this will certainly not benefit our negotiating position vis-à-vis Russia with regard to the problems at the eastern border of Europe. We are curious about what the Foreign Minister thinks about this and we are curious about his ideas on how to deal with this gordian knot. Then I'll come to the Middle East. That's what you really call a rough neighborhood. The well-known statement of "Chairman Mao" that power comes from the barrel of a gun is today in large parts of the Middle East and Africa the uncomfortable reality. The use of military force is not seen by the vast majority of the warring parties as an ultimatum remedium, to which you pass only after all diplomatic negotiations have failed. Precise negotiation is seen as an ultimatum remedium, which is only applied when military action has failed. So diplomacy is determined by the military facts on the battlefield and not the other way around. Of the warring parties, ISIS is by far the most dangerous group, convinced that it is destined to call the apocalypse predicted in the Bible and the Koran upon us. It is precisely this apocalyptic starting point that poses the risk that ISIS will not deter the use of chemical, and worse still, biological weapons. ISIS has already used chemical weapons on a number of occasions, albeit on a modest scale. On 12 March this year in Taza a small town 20 kilometers south of Kirkûk. Although no biological weapons have been deployed yet, there is evidence that this is certainly not an unthinkable scenario. Indeed, precisely the uncontrollability of the effects of biological weapons makes these weapons ideally suited to produce a worldwide apocalyptic effect. The WHO Scientific Committee concluded in a report published in June 2015 that the reproduction and even modification of the variola virus, the smallpox virus, by the application of synthetic biology today is relatively simple. In 2015, the AIVD's Joint Counterproliferation Unit and STUDY reported several times on the potential CBRN capabilities of non-state actors in particular in Syria and Iraq. In addition, a report by the WODC points out the use of drones to distribute CBRN weapons. A bizarre incident on April 22, 2015, in which a drone of radioactive material landed on the roof of the Japanese Prime Minister Shinzo Abe's house, clearly indicates that this is not a theoretical possibility. In addition, there are indications from several sources that ISIS is trying to develop or obtain these weapons. In this context, I would like to refer to the last NATO PA meeting in Tirana, where our colleague Senator Martens has published an excellent report, in particular on the threat posed by CBRN weapons. It endorses the fact that this threat is definitely increasing and that we cannot ignore it. On the other hand, in December, the NCTV indicated that the risk of the use of chemical, cytogenetic, radiological and nuclear resources by terrorists was not high. This is also apparent from the answer on 22 December 2015 of the questions asked in the Lower House on this subject. It seems that the government at the time felt that sufficient precautions had been taken. My group is not entirely reassured. What is reality and what is delusion? Our question to the Minister of Defence is therefore how it is currently assessing the CBRN threat and whether it has taken or should take further measures to better protect our military from this threat. Furthermore, the R&D capacity, the Research and Development capacity, has been virtually reduced in the CBRN area. Does the Minister see any reason to repair this capacity? I am referring here to research capacity. Then I come to migratory flows towards Europe. This issue has already been widely discussed in the recent debate on Europe. So I can be brief. There is an unprecedented migration of people in the world of about 60 million people. 30 million of them are located in a geographical area where migration towards Europe is the most obvious option. There is therefore also a security dimension to this. If we do not get a grip on these migratory flows, there is a risk that the social and political stability of the European continent will be undermined. The first signs that this is a real possibility are already there. It's the uncomfortable reality. The Turkey deal in combination with the hotspots in Greece is insufficient to regulate the expected increasing flow of migrants from Africa. For possible solutions it is inevitable to make agreements with a number of North African countries. We know from recent press releases that the Minister for Foreign Affairs is working on this within the framework of the EU. Our question to the Minister is how this negotiation process is going and how he estimates the chance that effective agreements can be reached with our neighbours in North Africa. The deterioration of the international security situation outlined above also affects the extent and nature of our defence effort. The armed forces need to be modernised and adapted to the fluid and unpredictable security situation. However, this adjustment and modernisation has been postponed over and over the last few years because for decades successive Cabinets have been draining many billions of euros from the defence budget. This has caused serious damage to the defence organisation. The armed forces are just a shadow of what they once were. From what we have left, the ability to continue has been severely compromised. Weirdly enough, however, in recent years the use of the weakened military force has only increased in recent years, and it is expected that in the future this use of the armed forces, both nationally and internationally, will only increase further. The cabinet has allocated extra money to the soul-blowing armed forces, but this extra money is not enough to turn the tide. After all, if the defence budget is raised again by EUR 200 to 300 million in 2017, then a billion euros has been added in the period 2012 to 2017. That seems to be an impressive amount, but the purchasing power of the defense has barely increased. Of course, I'm not telling the Secretary of Defense anything new. It has made the defence issue crystal clear on several occasions. I shall therefore confine myself to a few points which my group would like to highlight or which we still have questions about. The situation is downright worrying. In the opinion of the VVD Group in the planning period, a sum of around EUR billion per year is needed to put the basic rationale, investment and operational support in place. Mind you, this amount only allows for marginal investment. In fact, we're not getting any more armed forces for this amount, but we're just fixing what we thought we had. If the defence budget is not to be increased by a substantial amount of money, it is again necessary to cut back essential military capabilities. The downward adjustment of the level of ambition will then become inevitable and NATO agreements will no longer be possible. NATO has recently expressed itself very critically about the Dutch defence effort. Rightly so, because the Netherlands spends only 1.1% of GDP on defence, and if pensions and waiting payments and VAT payments are deducted from this, only 0.9% remains. This percentage is well below the NATO standard of 2%, but also below the European NATO average of 1.4% of GDP. It has been pointed out before not only by NATO but also by our own General Court. In its annual report on the results of the 2014 audit, the General Court stated that there is still an imbalance between tasks to be carried out, on the one hand, and staff, material and money, on the other hand, and concluded that "the Ministry of Defence has an inexhaustible change in itself'. In the audit of the Court of Auditors, which was presented to the House of Representatives on 18 May of this year, the Dutch Ministry of Defence was given another yellow card. In order to reverse the trend, substantial investment is needed over the next few decades, and that is precisely where he is at. In order to have a future-proof and relevant military force, it is important not only to invest enough money in Defence, but also to invest enough of the defence budget in new technologies and weapons systems. Within NATO, it has therefore been agreed that Member States should devote at least 20% of the defence budget to replacing equipment and investment in new techniques and weapons systems. This is the so-called investment ratio. However, the Netherlands also does not meet the standard for the investment ratio. The Netherlands last met this standard in 2009. Since then, a declining trend has started with the sad low 2013 with a percentage of 13.6 per cent. The estimates in recent budgets are based on a rapid increase in the investment ratio, but in practice this is disappointing. In 2015, the investment ratio was only 15%. An investment ratio of 21% was foreseen for 2016, but this is certainly not expected to be achieved. In fact, operating costs are necessarily offset by the introduction of investment. In addition, there is the fact that Defence is not compensated for price increases and currency risks. The organization is eating itself. The Minister presented this problem to the Study Group on Budget Space, which would present the results by this spring at the latest. Our question to the Minister is therefore whether the results are already known. In addition, the Defence Material Organisation, DMO, is understaffed and understaffed at other sites in the acquisition chain, which has led to underrealisation of the investment budget in recent years. Moreover, the DIP, the Defence Investment Plan, is not flexible. Sliding within the budget is tricky. What about the Minister's plans to increase the flexibility of the DIP? The Minister informed the House that, in view of the continuing underrealization, she decided to start from a five-year average for the investment ratio. Although this helps to narrow the gap between estimates and the realisation of investment expenditure, this obviously does not solve the real problem that defence resources and ambitions are not in balance. Our question to the Minister is what measures it has taken to resolve the under-employment of the DMO. What's the underemployment at the moment? What other measures has it taken to improve the functioning of the acquisition chain? We would also be grateful if the Minister could give us an insight into the extent of the commitments made at present and the extent to which they differ from the planning. What is the delta between the budget needed to maintain the armed forces and the available budget according to the multiannual budget? We are really approaching a crucial decision-making point with regard to our armed forces. We can't go on like this. The armed forces are eating themselves from the inside out. The current defence budget is totally inadequate. My group is therefore very concerned about the much-needed replacement of obsolete and worn-out weapons systems. After all, apart from the replacement of the elderly F-16 and the worn-out operational wheeled vehicles, a number of major replacement projects are coming towards us, which are also under budgeted. The current reservations for the three major maritime replacement projects, M-frigates, submarines and mine hunters, are a typical example of this. In order to make the Defence Investment Plan fit, these projects have never been compensated for price development and even reduced. It is therefore very doubtful whether the timely replacement of these capacities in the desired quality and quantity is still possible within the available budget. However, we cannot postpone the replacement of this equipment any further because of the fact that these units are approaching the so-called "end life of type," abbreviated ELOT, with rapid paces and, in the absence of replacement, they become not only technically but also operationally superfluous and unusable. The problem is that by constantly delaying necessary investments, a huge financial wave has arisen, which means that several projects need both budget and capacity at the same time. This leads to displacement effects and thus to the further advance of these projects and consequently to the further expansion of the financial wave. The Minister has also now had to shift investments to later years. We are therefore curious to see how the Minister will solve the problem of the worn-out weapons systems that will exceed their ELOT in the very near future. The acquisition of these systems takes a lot of time. Are we, even if the DOD gets money, too late to provide timely replacement? I'd like an answer from the Secretary of Defense. My input gives a slightly optimistic picture of the security situation. As has been known for some time, there are all kinds of emerging risks. Read our own 2010 inter-departmental study, the reconnaissance. Mr Knapen also took part. This study then talked about various uncertainties and risks. In the meantime, many of these risks have become real threats. The past 25 years have been characterized by a historically unusual period of geopolitical luwe. But this period of stability and peace is really over. We must reinvest an important part of the peace dividend collected in a military force capable of making a meaningful contribution on the eastern flank and south flank of Europe to the all-encompassing defence of our continent and to the protection of our citizens and infrastructure. In the east, this must be done mainly by credible deterrents, especially in the south, by continuing to regulate migratory pressure and to reduce and adapt the corresponding instability. I'm gonna close positive. Free Western societies have the money, technology, knowledge and population to successfully meet the challenges of the future. What has so far been lacking is the political will to devote a greater share of our prosperity to our security. The same applies to the Netherlands, one of the richest countries in the world, which is far below the mark in terms of defence. We can't look away anymore, because it's not five to twelve, it's two to twelve.

ParlaMint-NL_2016-05-31-eerstekamer-4.u110	Mr. Chairman. "Europe has never been so prosperous, so safe and so free." This is the opening line of the 2003 European Strategy Paper on International Security. The Balkan wars had ended, Europe could work on a better world. Europe's prosperity, security and freedom were presented as a result of a development; we now know that it was a snapshot. Our prosperity was under pressure from the financial crisis, our security because of what we now call the "edge of instability" for Europe and our freedom is no longer threatened by internal and external security risks. I would like to highlight four issues in this contribution on behalf of my group: the challenges facing the Netherlands and Europe in terms of international security, the EU's security strategy, the indispensable role of NATO and our efforts in defence. Our Constitution imposes the task on us to work for the international legal order. This provides the basis for an active foreign policy, which is increasingly focused on burning security issues and on Europe. There has once been hope of making the use of softpowers and a well-focused neighbourhood policy of the neighbouring parts of Europe a better world. In the east, however, it has awakened Russia's assertiveness and military build-up. Beyond the southern flank and in the Mediterranean, attempts at democratization have resulted in chaos. In Turkey, which we have given a role in Europe's border control, an autocratic regime is developing with monocultural traits. Further on in Africa, the drought once again disrupts entire societies and threatens lives of millions. My group is in favour of a 'comprehensive approach' and has often highlighted the link between the climate crisis, the economic crisis and the security crisis. Even now, my group's attitude is not that we withdraw into our own bastions, certainly not in our cherished welfare bastions, because, apart from the lack of solidarity that this speaks of, we are once again putting the horse behind the cart. If we are committed to an international legal order, we have a broad understanding of the law in mind. In biblical terms, this is called "shalom": peace through justice, development and freedom through commitment to one another, harmony through reconciliation. Those words may sound naive, but they are the only basis for our hope that our world will be okay. That's the hope we're doing politically. That hope also activates our responsibility, of the Netherlands and of Europe. In a month's time, Commissioner Mogherini will present the European Union's new security strategy. Expectations are high, although everyone knows how complicated it is to come up with a strategy with 28 Member States. But don't let this be another snapshot. If this strategy is truly global, it will have to identify longer-term objectives clearly and give substance to a "comprehensive approach." The flight of desperate people from Africa to Europe has to do with many factors, but the lack of potential for their own development in Africa is poignant and significant at the same time; significant for Europe, which knows its historical ties with Africa. The current situation must lead to new efforts to share prosperity and development opportunities. The Global Strategy of Europe is established "in close consultation with the Member States." My group would like to hear more about the Dutch commitment to the negotiations on this agreement. What we have achieved so far is quite general in nature and usually comes through the annotated agendas. Today's debate seems to us to be a good reason to talk about it in this House. What does the cabinet want with the European security strategy? We are also curious whether the community of values that Europe is or wants to be translates into a'security community', as Paul Scheffer calls it. This requires solidarity between Member States, including with regard to the reception of refugees. This also requires an awareness of the significance of political geography. Scheffer states in his beautiful essay "The Freedom of the Border" that limits define who we are. In this sense, the internal borders of Europe can never be definitively abolished. Scheffer is right when he says that Europe needs external borders to be something and at least to be a security community. That is what we are facing, both in the south and the east. The 2003 European security document was written before the accession of Eastern European countries to the European Union. At the time, it was carried by the Member States which had realised the internal market without frontiers. The Eastern European countries are different; their accession to the EU served as security for national independence and security and from an idea of borders that seemed to be overcome in Western Europe. The security strategy they need is local rather than global. What does this mean for their attitude and their commitment, even when it comes to Europe's commitment to global security and sustainable development goals? Is it also supported by the countries of Eastern Europe? How does the Secretary of State view this? In these new European relations there is also a message for us. Our solidarity is being called for with Eastern European countries which have entered a cold war atmosphere. The alliance that can and must ensure this solidarity is NATO. The important NATO summit in Warsaw in July promises to become an important one and will send a signal to Russia, if only because Ukraine will be there. How does the Minister assess the security situation on the northern and eastern flanks of Europe? Not only the Baltic countries, but also the Scandinavian countries are beginning to feel Russia's hot breath. At the same time, the dialogue with Russia must also continue. This is already mentioned in the previous contribution because a Russia that is becoming increasingly isolated from G8 to perhaps the Olympic Games can only become more revanchist. Solidarity with Eastern European countries in terms of security, and hence solidarity with NATO, must also be given a military expression. I think it is commendable that the Minister of Defense recently sent the NATO Defence Planning Capacity Review 2015/2016 to the Dutch Parliament for the first time, partly because of her argument that transparency is a great asset in the relationship between government and parliament. Can I ask the Minister to send documents of such a general defence interest directly to the First Chamber in future? You can see the commitment we are holding today, even when it comes to defence. NATO's comment doesn't lie about it. There is great concern about the downward trend in defence budget due to austerity policies since 2011. Now we are spending 1.16% of GDP on defence, while NATO is asking the Member States for 2%. By 2020, this would be 1.08% under unchanged policies. There is growing doubt in NATO about the employability of the Dutch armed forces. This report should be read as an expression of serious concern. Also what has been added in recent years is insufficient for the structural improvements needed. Investment in equipment is needed and land forces need to be brought up to level, including in the light of a possible threat in Eastern Europe. The Court of Auditors has also recently made it clear in an analysis that we are falling through lower limits and that the equipment management and, above all, the readiness, readiness and employability of the army are at stake. I'm not the first to quote this conclusion. The WRR and the AIV have also spoken out. The WRR recently stated that consistent defence targets and choices with associated multi-annual financial frameworks are needed. As early as April last year, the AIV expressed its views on this issue in the Opinion on Instability around Europe. It referred to a delta plan for the armed forces, more budget and the need to maintain an investment ratio of 20% of the military budget. Of course, I am now taking up my motion from December 2013. I have pointed out, by a majority in this Chamber, that the defence efforts of the Netherlands are lagging behind in the light of NATO's 2% standard. By expressing our desire to fulfil our obligations, we express our solidarity in the field of international and European security. The time when we could think that the end of the Cold War produced a peace dividend and we hardly needed an army anymore is really behind us. We simply have to live up to our obligations. It is now the case that the prosperous Europe is making Americans pay for their own security. That is not going well for long, partly in the light of discussions in America itself. My analysis is that in America at some point the question "can we still afford it and do we still want to be the safety umbrella for Europe in this way?" The reactions to my motion at the time pointed out new NATO agreements. Those appointments came through. A new commitment was issued in Wales in September 2014. The statement states: we will aim to move towards the existing guideline of spending 2% of GDP on defence within a decade. So in ten years' time, as members of NATO, we will move towards the 2% of GDP standard. This is an allergic language with the signature of the Netherlands underneath. In 2024, therefore, the Netherlands must have moved towards the 2% standard, but this year NATO, in the piece I just mentioned, notes that we are moving away from it. I am pleased that a number of groups here today are interested in this point. We can't go on like this. Something must change in the approach to our defence efforts. My group believes that we should have a plan with a multiannual financial perspective aimed at meeting the 2% standard or at meeting our commitments and obligations. It would be great if we could at least be clear about the way in which the government, not just the Minister of Defence, because we have already heard them, will act in this. Can we safely continue to consume as if we have three-and-a-half globes at our disposal, or will we not only have to drastically moderate our own consumption, but also our knowledge, our technique and our insight, and will we have to use our influence to include elsewhere the idea that the status quo is indigent? It only stirs up the insecurity in the world and therefore also puts our safety and that of our children in immediate danger. A debate on the international security strategy cannot be limited to military force, to the number of military interventions to which the Netherlands is capable or willing, to the capacity of the OSCE, or to the removal of terrorist threats. If we do not recognise the cause of international security problems, if we do not name and face the breeding ground of war, violence, climate change, drought and other scarcity, then we are looking at the world through old-fashioned safety glasses that seriously distort our world view and that we are suffering from a dangerous form of myopia. From a misplaced feeling of superiority we try to make refugees a pass in the place, while we know that stopping refugee flows without taking away the causes is similar to standing in a river with your arms wide and thus trying to stop the water, as Diederik Samsom said before he came up with the Samsom plan. The fact that we pretend that this can work, that Dutch politics embraced the Samsom plan and concluded an agreement with Turkey, the unworkability of which was clear even before the ink was dry, is mainly due to the forthcoming elections and the inconvenience that refugee flows bring with them in the still prosperous northern Europe. Our ancestors reported themselves as lucky seekers in places like Manhattan, Ambon, Ceylon, Celebes, Formosa, Malakka, Sumatra, Batavia, Curaçao, Brazil, Guyana, Suriname, the Gold Coast, the Virgin Islands, Tobago, Sint-Eustatius, Slaven Coast, the Cape and the Dutch East Indies. We did so from the famous VOC mentality of former Prime Minister Balkening. We took what we liked there: raw materials, goods and even people. The question of what right we did that played less at that time than is currently the case. But apparently the Dutch of that time felt superior to the underdeveloped "natives" in their eyes. We've seen a lot of the world, we've taken a lot of the world, and for that reason we owe a lot to the world. The Plan Bureau for the Environment calculated that the so-called "land footprint," the amount of land needed worldwide to provide for the Dutch consumption of citizens and government, the land use for the consumption of Dutch citizens, covers an area of three times the land surface of the Netherlands. Every night, 1 billion people go to bed hungry. And the hunger problem is advancing towards Europe. In Britain, one in five people live below the poverty line and around 1 million people go to bed every night with hunger. It is expected that the number of people in Western countries directly affected by hunger will increase rapidly through, among other things, the destruction of social services, unemployment and rising cost of living. Scientists from the United Nations Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change, the IPCC, expect millions of climate refugees to flee to the northern hemisphere in search of food this century. In 2013, there were 22 million people fleeing natural disasters, and this century the panel expects a huge increase due to climate change that causes misharvesting and rising geopolitical tensions as a result or in combination with it. Already we are seeing an increasing migration of people from Africa to countries such as Spain and Germany. That number will increase enormously if the food crisis deepens. The shortage of food was already at the root of the major uprisings in Tunisia and Egypt. According to the World Health Organisation, 2.2 million people die every year in developing countries from contaminated food and water. In addition to food shortages, soil erosion, extreme drought, deforestation, often combined with deep poverty and oppression, are the reason for people to migrate. We can hardly imagine that the shelves in the supermarket would be empty. However, there is a serious cause for concern, which is closely linked to the way in which our food supply is organised. Apparent excess in a very shaky balance. The picture of worried Chinese mothers and empty shelves in the Dutch supermarkets is about milk for Chinese infants... is just a small taste of what awaits us when large groups of consumers in emerging economies are going to take over our consumption pattern. Dutch food scientist Harry Aiking from Vrije Universiteit said: "In China, India and Brazil, hundreds of millions of people are working on the road up. And they can't be stopped with a cannon." Consumers in poor countries associate meat, dairy and eggs with prosperity, which means that demand for them will increase enormously and thus their price will increase. Poor people who are getting a little more prosperous do not need to come to Europe to make their new lifestyles impact on European eating and living patterns. KPMG estimates that food prices will increase by 70% to 90% by 2030. This means that there will not be enough affordable food to feed the world. We now know that war is not much further away than three missed hot meals. We are playing with fire with our agricultural policy: we are not feeding the hungry, we are feeding the hunger. We in the Netherlands will have to become less dependent on abroad for our food supply. There will be an end to the possibility of unbridled imports of feed for meat, milk and eggs from countries in South America or elsewhere. If food and raw materials become scarcer and more expensive, the main problem in the food producing countries will be how to feed their own population. Otherwise there will be hunger riots there. The question is why in the western world there seems to be little thought about these kinds of problems. The financial crisis of 2008 should have woken us up and made us aware that our food supply is at least as unstable as our financial system. The Netherlands is the most densely stocked country in the world. There are more farm animals than humans. And in that most cattle-proof country, Brabant is the most cattle-proof province. Of the 450 million animals slaughtered every year after a short and miserable life, a third, about 140 million animals, are bred in Brabant. This threatens the safety of citizens in the Netherlands. Yesterday it was announced that the official death toll among people due to the Q-fever epidemic, people who literally got sick from livestock farming in their surroundings, is not 26, as the official figures reported the day before yesterday, but 74. 74 deadly victims, because of Dutch goat farming alone, far more than terrorism has caused to victims in the Netherlands in recent years. The provincial board of Brabant set up a committee after the Q-fever disaster with the task of considering how to make our livestock farm sustainable. When presenting the committee's findings, the chairman, Daan van Doorn, said something very sensible: "The committee realizes that our current consumption pattern requires an equivalent of four globes." That sounds alarming. Four globes! While in the known part of the universe there is only one globe available that can contribute to the production of our diet. But Daan van Doorn also had a solution: we will therefore have to intensify agriculture further, build more megasthals to meet this growing demand. This is the only answer the architects of our current food system have to the question of how we can provide safe food for all people on our planet in the future, in a safe way that can prevent hunger and the associated war and molest. That is the further increase in production to meet demand. Not a multiplier of durable solutions, but a multiplication of the cause of the current problems. Doing the same thing over and over again and then expecting different outcomes. That's a definition that Joris Luijendijk gave of madness. The madness of our frozen food system fits perfectly. As Einstein said, you can't solve problems from the same way of thinking that caused the problems. Intensification of agriculture cannot make the earth's capacity more sustainable, let alone by adding the capacity of additional planets. We have seen this with the Q-fever epidemic where human health and animal life were literally sacrificed on the altar of the economy. The hunger that the world is currently experiencing is not due to the fact that we would produce too little food. On the contrary. Only two thirds of the food currently produced worldwide is actually eaten. Of the amount of food harvested on the fields, much and much less ends up in a human mouth. About 40% of the global grain harvest serves as food for the animals in the western bio-industry. Precious nutrients are literally swallowed by livestock farming, with a large proportion of the calories available being converted into body heat of the animal. Most of it is not available for human consumption. The security issue before us is mainly a food issue, more specifically a question of distribution. The most important question is: how do we ensure that all people can get sufficient food, both now and in the future? It's possible! But then we'll finally have to make real choices. Long-term choices. That is also why we strongly supported the Ganzevoort motion to strengthen the 3D approach. Not only on defence, but also on diplomacy and development. Development cooperation to achieve more self-reliance and thus less international tensions. Unfortunately, the implementation of this motion is hardly reflected in the current cabinet policy. We're gonna have to choose. Abraham Maslow summarized the choice between economic growth and security as follows: "Or you take a step forward to growth or you take a step back towards security." We can choose more of the same to end up with nothing. We can continue on the current road. The path of over-consumption in the West and increasingly in economically emerging countries, is to produce meat, dairy, eggs, fat products and sugars, which is a disaster both for our own health and for the health of our planet and for the welfare of animals in the livestock industry. That's the way of megasthals, of deforestation of tropical rainforests to make way for even more plantations for soy and corn to feed the animals in the livestock industry. This is also the path of the agricultural poison, which poisons soil and water and threatens to destroy bees and butterflies, the essential links in our eco- and food systems. The path of fertilizer, which can increase food production in the short term but in the long term leaves the soil bare and barren and which also costs a lot of fossil fuel to produce. The path of monocultures, allowing diseases and plagues to easily prevail, which means massive crop losses are lurking. The road through which five to seven farmers in the Netherlands have to stop their business every day because they are no longer able to sell their products below the cost price. Most of the beans that are in our grocery store in winter come from Africa. That we eat them instead of the people in Africa itself is because free trade is placed above all else and because we simply have more to spend on our food. Meanwhile, the remains of Dutch puff chickens, such as the wings, the legs and the intestines, are dumped on African markets at bottom prices. By effectively depriving countries of the right to protect their markets against this kind of unfair competition, we have also deprived countries of the right to take their own economy, including their own food system, into their own hands. This road leads to excess and waste of food on the one hand, and a very large shortage and exhaustion of the earth on the other. That is currently the greatest threat to security in the world, knowing that the world has enough to offer for everyone's needs, but not for everyone's greed. A safe world begins with an agricultural system that is in harmony with the environment, with the environment, human beings and animals, in which smart cooperation with nature is done instead of fighting it with agricultural poison. This is an agricultural system in which nutrients are closed on a regional scale, so that soils remain fertile without the use of fertilizer and in which food is no longer wasted, but actually feeds people. This not only means that we must prevent the loss of food between harvest and processing, between processing and supermarket and the disposal of food by consumers. Why does such a large proportion of the productivity of the fields in the south of the world end up in the western livestock industry and supermarkets? If we orientate the economy and food production much more regionally, we can prevent famines, preserve biodiversity and slow down climate change. In doing so, we are promoting security in the world more than fighter jets, mine sweepers or ground troops could ever be able to do so on their own. Moreover, we are building sovereignty of food, which we seem to have given up a long time ago, but which is the key to a sustainable, safe and just food system. Until recently, Olivier De Schutter was a special human rights rapporteur for the right to food at the United Nations. Food, like water, is a human right, but this right is actually being trampled on everywhere in the world. He took action against this and became a very influential advocate of agro-ecology. Agro-ecology is not only agriculture according to ecological principles, while ensuring greater food security by strengthening biodiversity and the ecosystem, but it concerns the whole food system, linking food production to the food chain and consumers. It is also aimed at the emancipation and autonomy of the farmer. The shooter pointed out to countries their duty to ensure that there is a sustainable food system in which everyone has enough to eat, now and in the future. In doing so, he rejected the bio-industry. Choosing agroecology is an ultimate form of opposition to the current economic system with free trade agreements such as NAFTA, CETA and the forthcoming TTIP. A system in which profit counts more than life. For almost a year De Schutter has been succeeded as human rights rapporteur for the right to food by Hilal Elver. Although it has only just begun this important task, it has already shown itself to fight tirelessly for a sustainable and just food system to promote food safety in the world. In the run-up to the 2015 Paris Climate Conference, she proposed dumping industrial thinking on agriculture, so that we can ultimately provide everyone with enough healthy food. The Dutch Government Scientific Council recently noted that the following issues in the field of food supply urgently need to be changed in the global context, including in the context of security policy. Agricultural policy must change into food policy, we must consume and produce less dairy and meat, food production must be more sustainable, an independent body must evaluate food policy and free trade must not prevent demands from being made on the ecological qualities of our food. I ask the Foreign Minister if the cabinet is prepared to make our food strategy an important part of the international security strategy. According to research by the University of Minnesota, we can feed 4 billion more people on the current arable land when we choose to stop processing agricultural crops into animal feed and biofuel. How could we ever deviate from the current form of production and consumption? Who is powerful enough to seriously tackle this issue and bring about the radical change of course needed in the context of international peace? There is an important role for the government. Consumers are often referred to, but they do not have the power to change the agricultural system, because Dutch agriculture focuses on the export of bulk products: 70% of everything we produce in Dutch agriculture goes abroad. The wretched thing is that it barely pays off the farmer, the family farm. Not here, not elsewhere. We know from history that wars are not uncommon about food, that wars cause food scarcity and migration flows, and that in times of war, we see that a safe existence is mainly linked to a safe food supply. Can the Foreign Minister indicate the role of food shortages, water shortages and climate change in international security issues, also in the light of the Ganzevoort motion adopted here? Finally, I have a question for the Foreign Minister. When will the cabinet respond to the association treaty rejected by the Dutch people? What is the way in which the government intends to deal with this proposal? According to what timetable? I'd like to get a response on this point.

ParlaMint-NL_2016-05-31-eerstekamer-4.u159	Mr. Chairman. I thank all the senators for their generous input into this debate. A lot of questions have been asked. There's 130 of them. This confirms the importance of security policy and its topicality. I think that is quite understandable. I think a lot of Dutch people now have a feeling of insecurity and an unintended feeling about what is happening in the world. Then it's mostly about terrorism. How do we control migration in a way that meets our standards and values? So it's good to answer all these questions. Perhaps I could make one introductory remark and concentrate on answering the questions. Earlier this year, we had a long debate here on the role of Europe. Is Europe a continent that exports stability or imports instability? In any case, we would like the first and try to limit the second as much as possible. This means that, in terms of security, we must pay great attention to the ring of instability surrounding Europe. In this sense, I believe that Europe has indeed become a rather fragile continent, hence the importance of the transatlantic link and the discussions on strengthening European peace and security policy. The question was rightly raised here about what that means for defence expenditure, missions and security expenditure. I think that is the first element on which the Dutch Government is focusing in its policy: the ring of instability around Europe. The second element has to do with what we protect in Europe. These are, of course, standards and values and human rights interests. Many questions have rightly been asked to the government about this. How do we defend ourselves against terror, against ISIS and against the risks posed by identity politics worldwide? What does that mean for Europe and for the protection of our values? It is clear that we are dealing with a very complex world, which is almost the cliché of the day, which means that there are a number of sliding panels, in which we have to determine our direction. That is a factual finding, not a value judgment. Then I come to the issue of the treaty. There are two basic principles to the UN Refugee Convention. I believe that is what Mr Schrijver asked me to do. The UN Refugee Convention does not concern economic migrants, because it is a global treaty. That is indeed the case. For your explanation, I would like to make the following point. The Refugee Convention covers refugees, as the title indicates. According to the Convention, refugees are, in summary, persons who have fled from well-founded fears of persecution for race, religion, nationality, belonging to a particular social group or political belief. Of course, economic migrants are not included. The second principle is the scope of the Convention. To this end, the Convention gives States that are parties the choice of applying it only to refugees from Europe or also to refugees outside Europe. In 1967, a protocol to the Convention was established to make its scope global in principle. However, the protocol leaves open the possibility of maintaining the geographical limitation if one so wishes. A handful of countries have made use of the possibility of geographical limitation. The Dutch Government did not do that. ParlaMint-NL_2016-05-31-eerstekamer-4.u206	That's right. Mr Van Apeldoorn is right that it does not contain all necessary means.
ParlaMint-NL_2016-05-31-eerstekamer-4.u207	But earlier it was said that they did not want to refer to that resolution, because it would not provide an international legal basis, which I believe has been established by many international law experts, at least it has been argued.
ParlaMint-NL_2016-05-31-eerstekamer-4.u208	Maybe I should give it a little more structure. I went down the bend a little fast. Firstly, there is the ban on violence in Article 2 (4) of the UN Charter. There are a number of exceptions to this ban. One of them is an invitation from a state to act on its territory. That is the legal basis for our efforts in Iraq. There's an invitation for that. Another exception is the right to self-defense, as laid down in Article 51 of the UN Charter. Provided that the conditions are met, any state may rely on self-defense. This also applies to Turkey and Saudi Arabia. I believe that is what a number of Members were asking for. Self-defense can be individual or collective. Collective self-defense constitutes the legal basis for Dutch action in Syria. Specifically, Iraq's collective self-defense against armed attacks by ISIS from Syria. An appeal to collective self-defense requires one or more armed attacks against a state from a third State. In the case of armed attacks originating from non-state groups, we are talking a great deal about the fact that the third state is not prepared or able to effectively stop these armed attacks. That is at the heart of the international legal legitimacy of our activities. Mr Van Apeldoorn referred to Security Council Resolution 2249. He's right. None of these resolutions contain any mandating for use of violence under Chapter 7. I think there can be no misunderstanding about that. They are not the legal basis either. So I didn't mean that one with the stacking. However, these resolutions are an important political signal from the Security Council and can be seen as an appeal to the collective responsibility of states. That brings me a little towards Mr Schrijver. They could be a stepping stone to a greater role for the UN Security Council, as the UN Security Council has been given primary responsibility for international peace and security in the UN Charter. For this reason, Article 51 provides that the right to self-defense ends when the UN Security Council has taken the necessary measures. I hope so. I would say that this is the process that we are seeing now, provided that there is agreement between the members of the Council, in particular the P5, on the implementation of that role. As you know, Russia has so far blocked any attempt to reach a chapter 7 resolution in the UN Security Council. Both bilaterally and in the support group, of course, we are talking to Russia about this kind of thing, so that you could slowly but surely go to such a situation, provided that it is politically desirable.

In my first term, I spoke extensively about the NATO expansion and what it has brought about. I said it was unwise. That is what we thought at the time, and that is what we think afterwards. The Minister replied that he thought that was a difficult discussion. I understand. I understand that he is not going to distance himself afterwards from NATO enlargement as it has happened. Now let's give the past a rest and look to the future. NATO has already expanded considerably in recent years. These are countries other than Albania or Montenegro. The 2008 decision, in principle, to give Ukraine and Georgia the prospect of membership, was controversial, including within NATO. Would it not be wise to withdraw this promise from 2008? With regard to the policy of sanctions against Russia, the SP Group is pleased to note that the Minister agrees with us that there are also problematic aspects to this, that at least there can be different views on this and that it is not clear that this has only positive effects, but that there is another side to this that we must keep in mind. I am pleased that the Minister continues to underline the temporary nature of these sanctions. We also fully agree with him that Russia should not violate the territorial integrity of other countries and that this has happened in the recent past. We would like to stress that this should apply to all countries, to all UN Member States, including Turkey, for example, where Syria's territorial integrity and action against Kurdish fighters are concerned.
